However, once you understand and imple- \n    51→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    52→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    53→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    54→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    55→below: \n    56→SURFACES MUST BE \n    57→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    58→II MUST BE \n    59→11 SMALL \n    60→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    61→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    62→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    63→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    64→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    65→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n    66→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n    67→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n    68→MATERIALS \n    69→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n    70→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n    71→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n    72→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n    73→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n    74→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n    75→solderability. \n    76→MACHINES \n    77→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n    78→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n    79→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n    80→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n    81→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n    82→able. \n    83→MANPOWER \n    84→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n    85→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n    86→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n    87→operating it. \n    88→METHODS \n    89→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n    90→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n    91→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n    92→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n    93→including all data that pertains to it. \n    94→ENVIRONMENT \n    95→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n    96→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n    97→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n    98→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n    99→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n   100→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n   101→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n   102→again may cause a defect. \n   103→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n   104→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n   105→Producing high quality solder joints \n   106→requires that five general process \n   107→variables need to be in control; the \n   108→materials, machines, manpower, \n   109→methods, and the environment. \n   110→2 Section I - Technology \n   111→\n   112→\n   113→=== PAGE 5 ===\n   114→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   115→I I I I I I 1; I :I I I I I \n   116→In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   117→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   118→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   119→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   120→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   121→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   122→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   123→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   124→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   125→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   126→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   127→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   128→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   129→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   130→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   131→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   132→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   133→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   134→there is considerable truth to what was said. \n   135→I \n   136→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   137→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   138→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   139→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   140→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   141→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   142→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   143→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   144→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   145→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   146→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   147→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   148→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   149→must first be defined and then maintained. \n   150→280- \n   151→260- \n   152→240- \n   153→T 220- \n   154→High quality, low defect soldering \n   155→requires identifying the optimum \n   156→temperature profile for reflowing \n   157→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   158→over and over. \n   159→I WETTING ; ‘ TIME , \n   160→30-60 .’ : SEC ~ \n   161→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   162→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   163→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   164→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   165→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   166→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   167→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   168→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   169→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   170→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   171→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   172→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   173→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   174→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   175→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   176→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   177→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   178→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   179→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   180→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   181→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   182→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   183→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   184→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   185→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   186→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   187→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   188→Section I - Technology 3 \n   189→\n   190→\n   191→=== PAGE 6 ===\n   192→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   193→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   194→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   195→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   196→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   197→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   198→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   199→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   200→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   201→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   202→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   203→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   204→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   205→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   206→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   207→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   208→three zones. \n   209→reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   210→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   211→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   212→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   213→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   214→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   215→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   216→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   217→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   218→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   219→set at the same value. \n   220→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   221→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   222→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   223→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   224→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   225→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   226→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   227→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   228→200 c \n   229→I PREHEAT1 I PREHEAT2 I REFLOW \n   230→I \n   231→I \n   232→I \n   233→I \n   234→I \n   235→I \n   236→I \n   237→I \n   238→I \n   239→I \n   240→I \n   241→COOLING I \n   242→TIME \n   243→4 Section I - Technology \n   244→\n   245→\n   246→=== PAGE 7 ===\n   247→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   248→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   249→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   250→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   251→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   252→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   253→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   254→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   255→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   256→SOLDER SPATTER \n   257→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   258→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   259→FICIENT DRYING. \n   260→BURNT BOARDS, \n   261→DEWET SOLDER \n   262→fi COLD SOLDER \n   263→JOINTS, DULL \n   264→SOLDER JOINTS \n   265→(TOO COLD) \n   266→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   267→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   268→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   269→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   270→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   271→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   272→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   273→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   274→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   275→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   276→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   277→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   278→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   279→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   280→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   281→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The \n   282→joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   283→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   284→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   285→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   286→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   287→DEFINE THE BAND \n   288→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   289→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   290→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   291→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   292→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   293→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   294→results. \n   295→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   296→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   297→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   298→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   299→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   300→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   301→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   302→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   303→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   304→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   305→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   306→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   307→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   308→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   309→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   310→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   311→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   312→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   313→must be exposed. \n   314→The total temperature deviation from \n   315→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   316→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   317→the band. \n   318→Section I - Technology 5 \n   319→\n   320→\n   321→=== PAGE 8 ===\n   322→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   323→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   324→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   325→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   326→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   327→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   328→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   329→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   330→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   331→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   332→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   333→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   334→AT = Q x AX t / (M x Cp) \n   335→WHERE: \n   336→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   337→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   338→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   339→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   340→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   341→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   342→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   343→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   344→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   345→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   346→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   347→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   348→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   349→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   350→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   351→profile. \n   352→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   353→J \n   354→mmmm \n   355→mmmm \n   356→mmmm \n   357→mmmm \n   358→mmmm \n   359→mmmm \n   360→OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   361→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   362→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   363→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   364→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   365→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   366→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   367→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   368→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   369→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   370→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   371→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   372→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   373→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   374→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   375→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   376→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   377→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   378→the figure. \n   379→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN \n   380→b \n   381→L \n   382→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   383→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   384→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   385→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   386→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   387→important consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \n   388→uniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \n   389→running a profile on a test board, then load the oven \n   390→down. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   391→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   392→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   393→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   394→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   395→and product loading. \n   396→6 Section I - Technology \n   397→\n   398→\n   399→=== PAGE 9 ===\n   400→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   401→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   402→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   403→and infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \n   404→modes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \n   405→easiest way to understand each of these heating mode \n   406→is through example. \n   407→CONVECTION \n   408→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \n   409→air, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \n   410→SMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \n   411→provides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \n   412→provides convective heating. Convection heating c \n   413→cooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \n   414→Only the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \n   415→is actually transferring heat. \n   416→2ONVECTION I \n   417→- HOT . \n   418→AIR \\ \n   419→FLOW - COLD OBJECT IS \n   420→- HEATED BY CONVECTION \n   421→FROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \n   422→CONDUCTION \n   423→Conduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \n   424→of different temperature are in contact with each other \n   425→A good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \n   426→burner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \n   427→contact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \n   428→occurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \n   429→tial exists within the mass. \n   430→Referring to a system as 100% convection or \n   431→100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \n   432→by convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \n   433→always present in signijicant quantities. \n   434→CONDUCTION \n   435→HEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \n   436→EQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \n   437→RADIATION \n   438→Infrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \n   439→ferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \n   440→example of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \n   441→is a non-contact heat transfer. \n   442→RADIATION I \n   443→In SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \n   444→phase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \n   445→the heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \n   446→convection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \n   447→dominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \n   448→fer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \n   449→in the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \n   450→dominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n   451→80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \n   452→product during the heating process. Heat is transferred \n   453→through the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \n   454→Section I - Technology 7 \n   455→\n   456→\n   457→=== PAGE 10 ===\n   458→HOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n   459→95.38 \n   460→Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \n   461→different temperatures are in sight of each other. The \n   462→heat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n   463→1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \n   464→of infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \n   465→the wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \n   466→the absolute temperature of the object. As the source \n   467→temperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \n   468→exponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \n   469→temperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \n   470→the source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \n   471→SMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \n   472→as not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \n   473→these low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n   474→(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \n   475→wavelength ranges. \n   476→111111lI \n   477→INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \n   478→In order to understand what parameters are important in \n   479→infrared heating, one can consider the general equation \n   480→for heat transfer between the heat source and the object \n   481→being heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \n   482→equation, but rather to point out the significance of what \n   483→is involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \n   484→follows: \n   485→Q = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \n   486→WHERE: \n   487→Q = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \n   488→V = Geometric view factor (0-1) \n   489→Es = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \n   490→At = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \n   491→K = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \n   492→Ts = Source temperature (OK) \n   493→Tt = Target temperature (OK) \n   494→(5.67 x w/cm2/”K4) \n   495→Absorption characteristics of solder \n   496→paste and circuit board material makes \n   497→IR an efficient means of heating. \n   498→The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \n   499→that leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \n   500→the oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \n   501→in the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \n   502→view factor comes in product design. If two very large \n   503→components are in close proximity to each other, the \n   504→view factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \n   505→which makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \n   506→f IRSOURCE \n   507→THIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \n   508→ADJACENT COMPONENTS \n   509→RESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \n   510→FACTOR \n   511→The source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \n   512→are in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \n   513→tions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \n   514→energy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \n   515→as they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \n   516→board material, solder paste, and the components all \n   517→absorb quite well. Providing that the overall source \n   525→emission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \n   526→repeatability. \n   527→8 Section I - Technology \n   528→\n   529→\n   530→=== PAGE 11 ===\n   531→HOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \n   532→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \n   533→temperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \n   534→ture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \n   535→heated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \n   536→be cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \n   537→tion is to look at the convection equation and note what \n   538→is important and what is not important. \n   539→CONVECTION EQUATION \n   540→The following is the general equation for convection \n   541→heating or cooling: \n   542→Q=HxAT \n   543→WHERE: \n   544→Q = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \n   545→H = Convective film coefficient \n   546→AT = Temperature difference between \n   547→(W/cm2-”C) \n   548→fluid and object (“C) \n   549→The amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \n   550→either changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \n   551→temperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \n   552→temperature will increase the temperature difference, \n   553→and thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \n   554→proportionally. \n   555→The convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \n   556→impact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \n   557→related to a number of variables, including velocity of \n   558→the flow and angle of attack of the flow. \n   559→Increasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n   560→“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \n   561→low velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \n   562→nice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n   563→~ ~~ ~~ \n   564→Perpendicular flow convection breaks \n   565→up surface thermal boundary layers, \n   566→which enhances heat transfer. \n   567→very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \n   568→a higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \n   569→more effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \n   570→cations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \n   571→the amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \n   572→tical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \n   573→components to shift around. The flow direction also has \n   574→a significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \n   575→is shown below: \n   576→PARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \n   577→I FLOW DIRECTION 4 \n   578→- \n   579→BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   580→CAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \n   581→BOARD, WHICH REDUCES \n   582→HEAT TRANSFER \n   583→Note that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \n   584→referred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \n   585→layer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \n   586→heat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \n   587→the boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \n   588→freely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \n   589→perpendicular flow convection: \n   590→PERPENDICULAR FLOW \n   591→FLOW DIRECTION \n   592→ttt \n   593→NO BOUNDARY \n   594→LAYER \n   595→NO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   596→ENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \n   597→TRANSFER \n   598→In the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \n   599→there is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \n   600→perpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \n   601→cooling results. \n   602→Section I - Technology 9 \n   603→\n   604→\n   605→=== PAGE 12 ===\n   606→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \n   607→In SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \n   608→pure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n   609→100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \n   610→over the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \n   611→in not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \n   612→would need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \n   613→the case. However, some systems are more dominated by \n   614→convection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \n   615→source. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \n   616→convection dominated machine as a convection ma- \n   617→chine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \n   618→CONVECTION \n   619→Both convection and IR have their unique advantages \n   620→and limitations. In convective systems, typically the \n   621→temperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \n   622→ture of the product in a given zone. This has the \n   623→advantage in that the product temperature will never \n   624→exceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \n   625→heating a product that has a large difference in the mass \n   626→of the components and distribution. In this case, the \n   627→larger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \n   628→components will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \n   629→is not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \n   630→heat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \n   631→may be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \n   632→efficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \n   633→CONVECTION \n   634→Less sensitive to shading \n   635→Heats more uniformly \n   636→AT (Source-target) is small \n   637→Low thermal mass of air requires \n   638→high flow rates to transfer heat \n   639→Inefficient (20 to 30%) \n   640→Will heat leads first, increasing \n   641→chance of solder wicking. \n   642→is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \n   643→loop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \n   644→reheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \n   645→limitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \n   646→levels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \n   647→that if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \n   648→soldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \n   649→product becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \n   650→ucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \n   651→as typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \n   652→IR \n   653→IR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \n   654→efficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \n   655→solder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \n   656→have the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \n   657→needed. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \n   658→dense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \n   659→limitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \n   660→between the source and the product is required to transfer \n   661→the heat. Products with high component mass differen- \n   662→tial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \n   663→to accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \n   664→reflowing products with component sizes up to and \n   665→including 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \n   666→arrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \n   667→system. \n   668→IR \n   669→Control source temperature only \n   670→High efficiency (60-70%) \n   671→Easy to control rate of rise \n   672→More sensitive to shading \n   673→Less uniform high mass differences \n   674→AT (Source-target) is large \n   675→10 Section I - Technology \n   676→\n   677→\n   678→=== PAGE 13 ===\n   679→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n   680→~ \n   681→Ease of cleaning flux \n   682→residues (RMA) \n   683→Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \n   684→a significant process consideration. The issue is really \n   685→not the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \n   686→to reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \n   687→the presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \n   688→generally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \n   689→soldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \n   690→fluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \n   691→future trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \n   692→paste. The future trends include two major process \n   693→changes that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \n   694→atmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n   695→500 ppm \n   696→FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \n   697→Fine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \n   698→to obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \n   699→sizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \n   700→particles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \n   701→volume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \n   702→RATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \n   703→RATIO = 6 / D \n   704→WHERE: \n   705→D = Particle Diameter \n   706→FOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \n   707→GREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \n   708→WITH LARGER PARTICLES. \n   709→Thus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \n   710→With more available surface area in the solder paste, \n   711→there is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \n   712→mind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \n   713→the temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \n   714→and the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \n   715→that one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \n   716→This can only be done through proper oven design. \n   717→NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \n   718→The second technology change that is driving the need \n   719→towards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \n   720→clean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \n   721→is creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \n   722→requires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \n   723→nents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \n   724→process must not add any significant oxidation to the \n   725→process. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \n   726→aggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \n   727→cleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \n   728→product. These solder balls eventually can create short \n   729→circuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \n   730→in a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \n   731→bility of creating solder balls. \n   732→A good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \n   733→ment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \n   734→National Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \n   735→conducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \n   736→the process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \n   737→listed below: \n   738→DESCRIPTION I PURITY LEVEL I \n   739→Significant reduction \n   740→in board discoloration \n   741→10,000 ppm \n   742→Reduction in solder 200 ppm \n   743→balling (RMA) \n   744→Reflow of low-residue \n   745→solder paste \n   746→< 100 ppm \n   747→The paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \n   748→show the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \n   749→ing flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \n   750→cleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \n   751→ture. It is recommended reading if your process may \n   752→require the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \n   753→nitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \n   754→using nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \n   755→mine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \n   756→your process. \n   757→Section I - Technology 11 \n   758→\n   759→\n   760→=== PAGE 14 ===\n   761→1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \n   762→WHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \n   763→When selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \n   764→into consideration all aspects that may affect the process \n   765→and production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \n   766→one issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \n   767→lead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \n   768→steps in the selection process are described in the follow- \n   769→ing paragraphs. \n   770→DEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \n   771→The first step is to define the acceptable process profile \n   772→band width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \n   773→accurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \n   774→is too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \n   775→negatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \n   776→show up in the process. The band width should be \n   777→defined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \n   778→profile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \n   779→detailed description. \n   780→DEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \n   781→The next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \n   782→mities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \n   783→mities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \n   784→and oven repeatability under production loading condi- \n   785→tions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \n   786→product on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \n   787→uniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \n   788→defined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \n   789→detailed description. \n   790→SIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \n   791→The oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \n   792→the pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \n   793→below: \n   794→LINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n   795→(MINIMUM) - LOAD FACTOR \n   796→Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   797→speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \n   798→onload area of the reflow oven. \n   799→Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one \n   800→issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \n   801→not lead you to the optimum decision. \n   802→In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \n   803→speed defined below must be greater than the minimum \n   804→calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \n   805→will not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \n   806→should be determined through testing or by the following \n   807→equation: \n   808→OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH PROCESS - \n   809→SPEED - PROCESS DWELL TIME \n   810→DEFINE PROCESS GAS \n   811→If a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \n   812→no-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \n   813→the purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \n   814→how the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \n   815→at the gas inlet. \n   816→REVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \n   817→The conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \n   818→looked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \n   819→define whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \n   820→required. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \n   821→processing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \n   822→most important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \n   823→for the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \n   824→product. \n   825→RELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \n   826→Once a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \n   827→remains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \n   828→structural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \n   829→designed to withstand the rigors of production? Review \n   830→the product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \n   831→Find out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \n   832→training available? These are the questions that must be \n   833→asked during the selection process. \n   834→INTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \n   835→Conveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \n   836→be an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \n   837→conveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \n   838→and-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \n   839→machines to a host and product flow control schemes if \n   840→needed. \n   841→12 Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n   842→\n   843→\n   844→=== PAGE 15 ===\n   845→HOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \n   846→DESCRIPTION \n   847→The selection steps listed on the previous page should \n   848→all be considered when comparing equipment. When \n   849→multiple ovens pass the selection criteria listed, then a \n   850→proper cost evaluation may be used to compare ovens. \n   851→This is especially important when comparing ovens that \n   852→utilize different heating technologies, as first cost and \n   853→operating costs can be significantly different. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \n   959→CONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \n   960→Convection oven thermal control requires tight control \n   961→of two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \n   962→maintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \n   963→that both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \n   964→MICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \n   965→be used as an example. The temperature and flow control \n   966→are defined specific to this system. \n   967→CONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \n   968→Temperature control of the system must consider both \n   969→the gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \n   970→ber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \n   971→walls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \n   972→total heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \n   973→the heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \n   974→The reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n   975→(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \n   976→controlled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \n   977→heating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \n   978→enters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \n   979→series of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \n   980→chromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \n   981→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM \n   982→AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n   983→II 4 Y \n   984→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n   985→heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \n   986→can occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \n   987→enters the process chamber through an array of holes. \n   988→The product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \n   989→and the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \n   990→couple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \n   991→repeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \n   992→tive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \n   993→constant conditions. \n   994→The flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \n   995→ducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \n   996→is pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \n   997→flow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \n   998→blower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \n   999→temperature control is accomplished in the same way as \n  1000→the dryout and reflow zone. \n  1001→The flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \n  1002→through the walls in an array of holes to break up the \n  1003→boundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \n  1004→arrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \n  1005→transfer. \n  1006→Convection oven thermal control requires tight \n  1007→control of two variables; temperature andflow. \n  1008→In order to maintain constant heat flux to the \n  1009→product, it is critical that both these variables \n  1010→are controlled. \n  1011→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 15 \n  1012→\n  1013→\n  1014→=== PAGE 18 ===\n  1015→CONVECTION OVEN WITH \n  1016→Convection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \n  1017→several reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \n  1018→required to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \n  1019→extremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \n  1020→mize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \n  1021→the degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \n  1022→a given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \n  1023→also required, which can result in a greater opportunity \n  1024→for oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \n  1025→heater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \n  1026→others. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \n  1027→job of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \n  1028→However, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \n  1029→rates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \n  1030→CHAMBER DESCRIPTION \n  1031→Many of the same principles apply for inerting when \n  1032→comparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \n  1033→FLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \n  1034→similar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \n  1035→brought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- \n  1036→VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1037→etary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \n  1038→entry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \n  1039→levels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \n  1040→controlled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \n  1041→in the system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1042→In order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \n  1043→where the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \n  1044→pure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \n  1045→zone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \n  1046→experiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \n  1047→cal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \n  1048→from these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \n  1049→Here, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \n  1050→gas is not detrimental to the process. \n  1051→Purity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \n  1052→maintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \n  1053→flow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \n  1054→per hour). \n  1055→GAS FLOW \n  1056→INPUT FROM \n  1057→N2 OR AIR \n  1058→SOURCE \n  1059→GAS FLOW \n  1060→INPUT FROM \n  1061→N2 OR AIR \n  1062→SOURCE \n  1063→16 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1064→\n  1065→\n  1066→=== PAGE 19 ===\n  1067→IR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \n  1068→One of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \n  1069→repeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \n  1070→influenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \n  1071→variable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \n  1072→pensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \n  1073→of repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \n  1074→nents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \n  1075→LAMP DEFINITION \n  1076→The closed loop heat flux control method used in a \n  1077→Research, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \n  1078→example of one type of control. The primary heat source \n  1079→in this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \n  1080→lamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \n  1081→quartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \n  1082→to keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \n  1083→lamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \n  1084→capacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \n  1085→exceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n  1086→20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \n  1087→the heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \n  1088→heating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \n  1089→is in the medium to long wavelength range. \n  1090→The greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \n  1091→response capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \n  1092→change occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \n  1093→librium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \n  1094→control, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \n  1095→instantly after its removal. \n  1096→CONTROL DEFINITION \n  1097→In order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \n  1098→heat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \n  1099→heat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \n  1100→wall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \n  1101→Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \n  1102→occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \n  1103→equilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \n  1104→closed loop control, as energy can be replaced \n  1105→in the heating chamber instantly after its \n  1106→removal. \n  1107→RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \n  1108→THERMOCOUPLE \n  1109→PRODUCT \n  1110→”‘7 \\ REFLF JON. $ RE-EMITTED IR \n  1111→VECTION 1 FLOW \n  1112→-- \\P \n  1113→PRODUCT \n  1114→Accurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \n  1115→of the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \n  1116→thermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \n  1117→regulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \n  1118→and convective components are collectively monitored \n  1119→by the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \n  1120→mocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \n  1121→percent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \n  1122→temperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \n  1123→equation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \n  1124→then the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \n  1125→chamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \n  1126→intensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \n  1127→operate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \n  1128→power is modified only enough to compensate for the \n  1129→bulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \n  1130→total heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \n  1131→compensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \n  1132→temperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \n  1133→repeatability off 2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \n  1134→is within this defined band. \n  1135→The ability to operate within this wide band has two \n  1136→significant advantages. The first is that the oven may \n  1137→be loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \n  1138→that profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \n  1139→Loading may begin when all oven temperatures are \n  1140→within f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \n  1141→achieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \n  1142→window was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \n  1143→operation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \n  1144→Profile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \n  1145→increases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \n  1146→decreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \n  1147→from a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \n  1148→changeover time is under 15 minutes. \n  1149→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 17 \n  1150→\n  1151→\n  1152→=== PAGE 20 ===\n  1153→IR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1154→PREHEAT TOP (IR1) \n  1155→Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \n  1156→accomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \n  1157→curs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \n  1158→the heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \n  1159→the benefits of convection, which includes improving \n  1160→populated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \n  1161→amount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \n  1162→required to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \n  1163→ment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \n  1164→convection oven. \n  1165→REFLOW \n  1166→TOP (IR5) DRY TOP (IR3) PREFLOW TOP (IR4) \n  1167→NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1168→The 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \n  1169→dominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \n  1170→environment at low flow levels. The system uses a \n  1171→proprietary gas flow distribution method through the \n  1172→chamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \n  1173→process nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \n  1174→chamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \n  1175→keep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \n  1176→sections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \n  1177→chamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \n  1178→oxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \n  1179→venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \n  1180→system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1181→PREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) \n  1182→The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \n  1183→used once and then expended. This has the advantage \n  1184→that process contaminants are not recirculated which \n  1185→helps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \n  1186→chamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \n  1187→elevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \n  1188→crank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \n  1189→coming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \n  1190→Flow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n  1191→(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \n  1192→field are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n  1193→(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \n  1194→hour (37 cubic meters per hour). These purity levels are \n  1195→measured with the machine fully loaded with product. \n  1196→REFLOW \n  1197→BOTTOM (1R6) DRY BOTTOM (IR3) PREFLOW BOTTOM (IR4) \n  1198→~~~ ~ \n  1199→The amount of flow in the oven is more \n  1200→related to the quantity required to accom- \n  1201→plish a high purity nitrogen environment, \n  1202→which is considerably less than in the case \n  1203→of a convection oven. \n  1204→NOTE: VENTURI EXHAUSTS ARE ACTIVATED BY COMPRESSED AIR, \n  1205→ALL OTHERS MAY BE NITROGEN OR COMPRESSED AIR. \n  1206→18 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1207→\n  1208→\n  1209→=== PAGE 21 ===\n  1210→EDGE CONVEYOR \n  1211→Proper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \n  1212→as typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \n  1213→the bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \n  1214→has lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \n  1215→product will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \n  1216→straightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \n  1217→As the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \n  1218→period, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \n  1219→system from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1220→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n  1221→(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \n  1222→of the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \n  1223→that runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n  1224→1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each \n  1225→rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \n  1226→of welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \n  1227→structural integrity. \n  1228→The use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \n  1229→rails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \n  1230→minimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \n  1231→As the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \n  1232→chine warm-up period, they will grow and \n  1233→tend to warp. Now let me create a comprehensive summary document with all the extracted specifications.\n\n## HANDBOOK SPECIFICATIONS EXTRACTION REPORT\n\nBased on my analysis of the handbook at `/app/data/handbook.pdf`, here are the critical specifications you requested:\n\n---\n\n### 1. **PREHEAT ZONE DEFINITION**\n\n**Temperature Range:** 100°C to 150°C\n- **Start Temperature:** 100°C\n- **End Temperature:** 150°C\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 5\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"In the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to 150°C.\"\n\n---\n\n### 2. **PREHEAT RAMP RATE**\n\n**Maximum Allowable Ramp Rate:** Less than 2°C/second\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 5\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"The most critical parameter in the preheat section is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2°C/second. The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast.\"\n\n**Formula for Ramp Rate Calculation:**\nThe handbook doesn't explicitly state a formula, but the standard calculation is:\n```\nRamp Rate (°C/s) = ΔT / Δt = (T₂ - T₁) / (t₂ - t₁)\n```\nWhere:\n- ΔT = Temperature change (°C)\n- Δt = Time change (seconds)\n- T₂, T₁ = Temperatures at two consecutive measurement points\n- t₂, t₁ = Times at two consecutive measurement points\n\n**Computation Method:** Calculate slope between consecutive thermocouple samples in the preheat temperature region (100°C - 150°C).\n\n---\n\n### 3. **TIME ABOVE LIQUIDUS (TAL)**\n\n**Definition:** The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting point (liquidus temperature)\n\n**Requirements:**\n- **Minimum TAL:** 30 seconds\n- **Maximum TAL:** 60 seconds\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 5\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting point is referred to as the 'wetting time'. The wetting time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in the joint, which result in brittle solder joints.\"\n\n**Note:** TAL and \"wetting time\" are used interchangeably in the handbook.\n\n**Threshold Temperature:** The liquidus temperature is solder-specific:\n- For 63/37 Eutectic solder: 183°C\n- For other solder types, use the solder-specific liquidus temperature\n\n---\n\n### 4. **PEAK TEMPERATURE REQUIREMENTS**\n\n**Minimum Peak Temperature:** Liquidus Temperature + 20°C\n\n**For 63/37 Eutectic Solder:**\n- Liquidus Temperature: 183°C\n- **Minimum Peak Temperature: 203°C**\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 5\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be exceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow.\"\n\n**Formula:**\n```\nMinimum Peak Temperature = Solder Liquidus Temperature + 20°C\n```\n\n**Relationship to Liquidus:** The peak must exceed the liquidus by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow.\n\n---\n\n### 5. **CONVEYOR SPEED REQUIREMENTS**\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 14\n\n#### **Board Geometry Requirements:**\n\n**Minimum Line Speed Formula:**\n```\nLINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = (BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD) / LOAD FACTOR\n```\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"LINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\"\n> \n> \"Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the onload area of the reflow oven.\"\n\n#### **Thermal Dwell Requirement:**\n\n**Process Speed Formula:**\n```\nPROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n```\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"PROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\"\n>\n> \"The process speed should be determined through testing or by the following equation...In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process speed defined below must be greater than the minimum calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks will not occur at the reflow oven.\"\n\n**Minimum Conveyor Speed Calculation:**\n```\nRequired Minimum Speed (cm/min) = (Effective Heated Length in cm / Minimum Dwell Time in seconds) × 60\n```\n\n**Critical Constraint:** The process speed (based on thermal dwell) must be **greater than** the minimum line speed (based on board geometry) to prevent bottlenecks.\n\n---\n\n### 6. **THERMOCOUPLE SELECTION**\n\n**Selection Criteria:** When multiple thermocouples are available, use thermocouples attached to:\n1. **The LARGEST device** (highest thermal mass component)\n2. **The SMALLEST device** (lowest thermal mass component)\n\n**Purpose:** To measure the thermal mass differential non-uniformity across the board\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 8\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"The non-uniformity caused by thermal mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a profile.\"\n\n#### **Additional Thermocouple Placement Guidance:**\n\nFor oven uniformity testing (Page 8):\n- Use a **bare board** with a **matrix of thermocouple points**\n- Include points on **all board edges**: front, back, left, and right\n- Include **center of board**\n\n**Recommended Analysis Approach:**\nWhen analyzing profile compliance:\n1. The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast.\"\n\n**Formula for Ramp Rate Calculation:**\nThe handbook doesn't explicitly state a formula, but the standard calculation is:\n```\nRamp Rate (°C/s) = ΔT / Δt = (T₂ - T₁) / (t₂ - t₁)\n```\nWhere:\n- ΔT = Temperature change (°C)\n- Δt = Time change (seconds)\n- T₂, T₁ = Temperatures at two consecutive measurement points\n- t₂, t₁ = Times at two consecutive measurement points\n\n**Computation Method:** Calculate slope between consecutive thermocouple samples in the preheat temperature region (100°C - 150°C).\n\n---\n\n### 3. **TIME ABOVE LIQUIDUS (TAL)**\n\n**Definition:** The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting point (liquidus temperature)\n\n**Requirements:**\n- **Minimum TAL:** 30 seconds\n- **Maximum TAL:** 60 seconds\n\n**Page Reference:** Page 5\n\n**Exact Handbook Text:**\n> \"The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting point is referred to as the 'wetting time'. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929","id":"msg_vrtx_019wEwHwKP9zf98quFnE9qe4","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01UHxv73gbyK6LAad16bS8Fx","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/app/process_reflow_data.py","content":"#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"\nReflow Machine Data Analysis Script\nAnalyzes thermocouple data, MES logs, and defect data for reflow profile compliance.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport pandas as pd\nimport json\nimport numpy as np\nfrom typing import Dict, List, Tuple\n\n\n# HANDBOOK SPECIFICATIONS\n# Based on handbook.pdf analysis\n\n# Preheat zone definition (Page 5)\nPREHEAT_START_TEMP = 100.0  # °C\nPREHEAT_END_TEMP = 150.0    # °C\n\n# Maximum preheat ramp rate (Page 5)\nMAX_PREHEAT_RAMP_RATE = 2.0  # °C/s\n\n# Time Above Liquidus (TAL) requirements (Page 5)\nMIN_TAL = 30.0  # seconds\nMAX_TAL = 60.0  # seconds\n\n# Peak temperature requirement (Page 5)\nPEAK_TEMP_OFFSET = 20.0  # °C above liquidus\n\n\ndef load_data():\n    \"\"\"Load all data files.\"\"\"\n    print(\"Loading data files...\")\n\n    mes_log = pd.read_csv('/app/data/mes_log.csv')\n    thermocouples = pd.read_csv('/app/data/thermocouples.csv')\n    defects = pd.read_csv('/app/data/test_defects.csv')\n\n    print(f\"  MES log: {len(mes_log)} runs\")\n    print(f\"  Thermocouple data: {len(thermocouples)} records\")\n    print(f\"  Defect data: {len(defects)} records\")\n\n    return mes_log, thermocouples, defects\n\n\ndef calculate_max_preheat_ramp_rate(tc_data: pd.DataFrame) -> Tuple[str, float]:\n    \"\"\"\n    Calculate maximum ramp rate during preheat zone (100-150°C) for a single TC.\n\n    Returns:\n        Tuple of (tc_id, max_ramp_rate_c_per_s)\n    \"\"\"\n    # Filter data in preheat zone\n    preheat_data = tc_data[\n        (tc_data['temp_c'] >= PREHEAT_START_TEMP) &\n        (tc_data['temp_c'] <= PREHEAT_END_TEMP)\n    ].sort_values('time_s')\n\n    if len(preheat_data) < 2:\n        return tc_data['tc_id'].iloc[0], 0.0\n\n    # Calculate ramp rates between consecutive points\n    temps = preheat_data['temp_c'].values\n    times = preheat_data['time_s'].values\n\n    ramp_rates = []\n    for i in range(1, len(temps)):\n        dt = times[i] - times[i-1]\n        if dt > 0:\n            dtemp = temps[i] - temps[i-1]\n            ramp_rate = dtemp / dt\n            ramp_rates.append(ramp_rate)\n\n    max_ramp = max(ramp_rates) if ramp_rates else 0.0\n    tc_id = preheat_data['tc_id'].iloc[0]\n\n    return tc_id, max_ramp\n\n\ndef process_question1(mes_log: pd.DataFrame, tc_data: pd.DataFrame) -> Dict:\n    \"\"\"\n    Q1: Calculate maximum temperature ramp rate during preheat for each run.\n    Use the most representative thermocouple (largest mass device as per handbook).\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n=== Processing Question 1: Preheat Ramp Rate ===\")\n\n    results = {\n        \"ramp_rate_limit_c_per_s\": MAX_PREHEAT_RAMP_RATE,\n        \"violating_runs\": [],\n        \"max_ramp_by_run\": {}\n    }\n\n    # Get all unique runs\n    all_runs = sorted(mes_log['run_id'].unique())\n\n    for run_id in all_runs:\n        run_tc_data = tc_data[tc_data['run_id'] == run_id]\n\n        if len(run_tc_data) == 0:\n            # No thermocouple data for this run\n            continue\n\n        # According to handbook, use thermocouple on largest mass component\n        # The tc_location field indicates 'largest_mass' or 'smallest_mass'\n        largest_mass_data = run_tc_data[run_tc_data['tc_location'] == 'largest_mass']\n\n        if len(largest_mass_data) > 0:\n            # Use largest mass thermocouple\n            tc_id, max_ramp = calculate_max_preheat_ramp_rate(largest_mass_data)\n        else:\n            # Fallback: use first available thermocouple\n            tc_groups = run_tc_data.groupby('tc_id')\n            tc_id = list(tc_groups.groups.keys())[0]\n            tc_id, max_ramp = calculate_max_preheat_ramp_rate(tc_groups.get_group(tc_id))\n\n        results[\"max_ramp_by_run\"][run_id] = {\n            \"tc_id\": tc_id,\n            \"max_preheat_ramp_c_per_s\": round(max_ramp, 2)\n        }\n\n        # Check if violating\n        if max_ramp > MAX_PREHEAT_RAMP_RATE:\n            results[\"violating_runs\"].append(run_id)\n\n    # Sort violating runs\n    results[\"violating_runs\"] = sorted(results[\"violating_runs\"])\n\n    print(f\"  Analyzed {len(results['max_ramp_by_run'])} runs\")\n    print(f\"  Violating runs: {len(results['violating_runs'])}\")\n\n    return results\n\n\ndef calculate_tal(tc_data: pd.DataFrame, liquidus_temp: float) -> float:\n    \"\"\"\n    Calculate Time Above Liquidus (TAL) in seconds.\n\n    Args:\n        tc_data: Thermocouple data for a single run and TC\n        liquidus_temp: Solder liquidus temperature in °C\n\n    Returns:\n        TAL in seconds\n    \"\"\"\n    # Filter data above liquidus temperature\n    above_liquidus = tc_data[tc_data['temp_c'] > liquidus_temp].sort_values('time_s')\n\n    if len(above_liquidus) == 0:\n        return 0.0\n\n    # Find continuous segments above liquidus\n    times = above_liquidus['time_s'].values\n\n    if len(times) == 0:\n        return 0.0\n\n    # Simple approach: time from first to last point above liquidus\n    # More accurate: sum of time intervals\n    total_tal = 0.0\n\n    # Check for continuous segments\n    for i in range(len(times) - 1):\n        time_diff = times[i+1] - times[i]\n        # If consecutive points (assume 10s sampling interval)\n        if time_diff <= 20:  # Allow some tolerance\n            total_tal += time_diff\n\n    return total_tal\n\n\ndef process_question2(mes_log: pd.DataFrame, tc_data: pd.DataFrame) -> List[Dict]:\n    \"\"\"\n    Q2: Calculate Time Above Liquidus (TAL) for each run.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n=== Processing Question 2: Time Above Liquidus (TAL) ===\")\n\n    results = []\n\n    # Get all unique runs\n    all_runs = sorted(mes_log['run_id'].unique())\n\n    for run_id in all_runs:\n        run_info = mes_log[mes_log['run_id'] == run_id].iloc[0]\n        liquidus_temp = run_info['solder_liquidus_c']\n\n        run_tc_data = tc_data[tc_data['run_id'] == run_id]\n\n        if len(run_tc_data) == 0:\n            continue\n\n        # Use largest mass thermocouple as per handbook\n        largest_mass_data = run_tc_data[run_tc_data['tc_location'] == 'largest_mass']\n\n        if len(largest_mass_data) > 0:\n            tc_id = largest_mass_data['tc_id'].iloc[0]\n            tal = calculate_tal(largest_mass_data, liquidus_temp)\n        else:\n            # Fallback: use first available thermocouple\n            tc_groups = run_tc_data.groupby('tc_id')\n            tc_id = list(tc_groups.groups.keys())[0]\n            tc_group_data = tc_groups.get_group(tc_id)\n            tal = calculate_tal(tc_group_data, liquidus_temp)\n\n        # Determine compliance status\n        status = \"compliant\" if (MIN_TAL <= tal <= MAX_TAL) else \"non-compliant\"\n\n        results.append({\n            \"run_id\": run_id,\n            \"tc_id\": tc_id,\n            \"tal_s\": round(tal, 2),\n            \"required_min_tal_s\": MIN_TAL,\n            \"required_max_tal_s\": MAX_TAL,\n            \"status\": status\n        })\n\n    print(f\"  Analyzed {len(results)} runs\")\n    compliant = sum(1 for r in results if r['status'] == 'compliant')\n    print(f\"  Compliant runs: {compliant}/{len(results)}\")\n\n    return results\n\n\ndef get_peak_temperature(tc_data: pd.DataFrame) -> float:\n    \"\"\"Get peak (maximum) temperature from thermocouple data.\"\"\"\n    if len(tc_data) == 0:\n        return 0.0\n    return tc_data['temp_c'].max()\n\n\ndef process_question3(mes_log: pd.DataFrame, tc_data: pd.DataFrame) -> Dict:\n    \"\"\"\n    Q3: Check whether each run meets peak temperature requirement.\n    Requirement: Peak temp >= Liquidus + 20°C\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n=== Processing Question 3: Peak Temperature ===\")\n\n    results = {\n        \"failing_runs\": [],\n        \"min_peak_by_run\": {}\n    }\n\n    all_runs = sorted(mes_log['run_id'].unique())\n\n    for run_id in all_runs:\n        run_info = mes_log[mes_log['run_id'] == run_id].iloc[0]\n        liquidus_temp = run_info['solder_liquidus_c']\n        required_min_peak = liquidus_temp + PEAK_TEMP_OFFSET\n\n        run_tc_data = tc_data[tc_data['run_id'] == run_id]\n\n        if len(run_tc_data) == 0:\n            # No thermocouple data - run is failing\n            results[\"failing_runs\"].append(run_id)\n            results[\"min_peak_by_run\"][run_id] = {\n                \"tc_id\": None,\n                \"peak_temp_c\": None,\n                \"required_min_peak_c\": round(required_min_peak, 2)\n            }\n            continue\n\n        # Use largest mass thermocouple (worst case - coldest spot)\n        largest_mass_data = run_tc_data[run_tc_data['tc_location'] == 'largest_mass']\n\n        if len(largest_mass_data) > 0:\n            tc_id = largest_mass_data['tc_id'].iloc[0]\n            peak_temp = get_peak_temperature(largest_mass_data)\n        else:\n            # Fallback: use first available thermocouple\n            tc_groups = run_tc_data.groupby('tc_id')\n            tc_id = list(tc_groups.groups.keys())[0]\n            peak_temp = get_peak_temperature(tc_groups.get_group(tc_id))\n\n        results[\"min_peak_by_run\"][run_id] = {\n            \"tc_id\": tc_id,\n            \"peak_temp_c\": round(peak_temp, 2),\n            \"required_min_peak_c\": round(required_min_peak, 2)\n        }\n\n        # Check if failing\n        if peak_temp < required_min_peak:\n            results[\"failing_runs\"].append(run_id)\n\n    results[\"failing_runs\"] = sorted(results[\"failing_runs\"])\n\n    print(f\"  Analyzed {len(results['min_peak_by_run'])} runs\")\n    print(f\"  Failing runs: {len(results['failing_runs'])}\")\n\n    return results\n\n\ndef process_question4(mes_log: pd.DataFrame) -> List[Dict]:\n    \"\"\"\n    Q4: Check whether conveyor speed in each run is feasible.\n\n    Based on handbook Page 14:\n    - Minimum line speed is based on board geometry and throughput\n    - The actual conveyor speed should be checked against thermal requirements\n\n    For this analysis, we'll use a simplified approach based on the data available.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n=== Processing Question 4: Conveyor Speed Feasibility ===\")\n\n    results = []\n\n    all_runs = sorted(mes_log['run_id'].unique())\n\n    for run_id in all_runs:\n        run_info = mes_log[mes_log['run_id'] == run_id].iloc[0]\n\n        # Get actual conveyor speed from MES log\n        actual_speed = run_info['conveyor_speed_cm_min']\n\n        # Calculate required minimum speed based on board geometry\n        # From handbook: LINE SPEED (MIN) = BOARDS/MIN × LENGTH/BOARD / LOAD_FACTOR\n        boards_per_min = run_info['boards_per_min']\n        board_length = run_info['board_length_cm']\n        loading_factor = run_info['loading_factor']\n\n        if loading_factor > 0:\n            required_min_speed = (boards_per_min * board_length) / loading_factor\n        else:\n            required_min_speed = 0.0\n\n        # Check if actual speed meets requirement\n        meets = actual_speed >= required_min_speed\n\n        results.append({\n            \"run_id\": run_id,\n            \"required_min_speed_cm_min\": round(required_min_speed, 2),\n            \"actual_speed_cm_min\": round(actual_speed, 2),\n            \"meets\": meets\n        })\n\n    print(f\"  Analyzed {len(results)} runs\")\n    meeting = sum(1 for r in results if r['meets'])\n    print(f\"  Meeting requirements: {meeting}/{len(results)}\")\n\n    return results\n\n\ndef process_question5(mes_log: pd.DataFrame, defects: pd.DataFrame, q1_results: Dict,\n                      q2_results: List[Dict], q3_results: Dict, q4_results: List[Dict]) -> List[Dict]:\n    \"\"\"\n    Q5: Select the best run per board_family based on quality and efficiency.\n\n    Criteria for \"best\" run:\n    1. Highest yield (quality)\n    2. Profile compliance (Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4)\n    3. Highest throughput (efficiency)\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n=== Processing Question 5: Best Run Selection ===\")\n\n    # Create compliance lookup dictionaries\n    q2_lookup = {r['run_id']: r['status'] for r in q2_results}\n    q3_failing = set(q3_results['failing_runs'])\n    q4_lookup = {r['run_id']: r['meets'] for r in q4_results}\n    q1_violating = set(q1_results['violating_runs'])\n\n    # Get yield data from defects\n    yield_data = defects[defects['inspection_stage'] == 'SUMMARY'].copy()\n    yield_dict = {}\n    for _, row in yield_data.iterrows():\n        if pd.notna(row['fp_yield_pct']):\n            yield_dict[row['run_id']] = row['fp_yield_pct']\n\n    results = []\n\n    # Group runs by board_family\n    board_families = sorted(mes_log['board_family'].unique())\n\n    for board_family in board_families:\n        family_runs = mes_log[mes_log['board_family'] == board_family]\n\n        # Score each run\n        run_scores = []\n\n        for _, run_info in family_runs.iterrows():\n            run_id = run_info['run_id']\n\n            # Quality score (0-100 from yield)\n            quality_score = yield_dict.get(run_id, 0.0)\n\n            # Compliance score (0 or 1 for each criterion)\n            compliance_score = 0\n            if run_id not in q1_violating:\n                compliance_score += 1\n            if q2_lookup.get(run_id) == 'compliant':\n                compliance_score += 1\n            if run_id not in q3_failing:\n                compliance_score += 1\n            if q4_lookup.get(run_id, False):\n                compliance_score += 1\n\n            # Efficiency score (boards per minute)\n            efficiency_score = run_info['boards_per_min']\n\n            # Combined score: prioritize compliance, then quality, then efficiency\n            combined_score = (compliance_score * 1000) + quality_score + (efficiency_score * 0.1)\n\n            run_scores.append({\n                'run_id': run_id,\n                'score': combined_score,\n                'quality': quality_score,\n                'compliance': compliance_score,\n                'efficiency': efficiency_score\n            })\n\n        # Sort by score (descending)\n        run_scores.sort(key=lambda x: x['score'], reverse=True)\n\n        # Best run is the highest scoring\n        best_run = run_scores[0]['run_id']\n        runner_ups = [r['run_id'] for r in run_scores[1:]]\n\n        results.append({\n            \"board_family\": board_family,\n            \"best_run_id\": best_run,\n            \"runner_up_run_ids\": runner_ups\n        })\n\n    print(f\"  Analyzed {len(results)} board families\")\n\n    return results\n\n\ndef main():\n    \"\"\"Main processing function.\"\"\"\n    print(\"=\" * 80)\n    print(\"REFLOW MACHINE DATA ANALYSIS\")\n    print(\"=\" * 80)\n\n    # Load data\n    mes_log, thermocouples, defects = load_data()\n\n    # Process Question 1\n    q1_results = process_question1(mes_log, thermocouples)\n    with open('/app/output/q01.json', 'w') as f:\n        json.dump(q1_results, f, indent=2)\n    print(\"  ✓ Saved to /app/output/q01.json\")\n\n    # Process Question 2\n    q2_results = process_question2(mes_log, thermocouples)\n    with open('/app/output/q02.json', 'w') as f:\n        json.dump(q2_results, f, indent=2)\n    print(\"  ✓ Saved to /app/output/q02.json\")\n\n    #
